University & AcAdemics
BYU provides an opportunity for athletes

to grow mentally, physically and spiritu-

ally. BYU’s honor code requires a drug

and alcohol-free commitment from its

students, helping them to focus on aca-

demic and athletic endeavors. Year after

year BYU leads the Mountain West in

the number of national and conference

academic honors.

30 yeArs Of cHAmpiOnsHips
For BYU football, the past 31 years have

been packed full of championships, bowl

games and national honors. Since 1974

BYU has played in 24 bowl games, won

one national championship and 20 con-

ference championships and earned 14

national top-25 finishes, including four

top-10 finishes. Cougar football has a

long-established tradition of winning.

scHedULe & OppOnents
BYU traditionally schedules one of the

toughest non-conference schedules in

the country. In the last 15 years BYU

has earned victories over Miami (Fla.),

Notre Dame, Oklahoma, Washington,

Penn State, Cal, Texas A&M, Virginia,

Arizona State and Kansas State. Upcoming

opponents include: Florida State, UCLA,

Arizona State, Arizona, Washington.

nAtiOnAL tv eXpOsUre
With 35 nationally and regionally tele-

vised games over the last six years,

BYU football is in the spotlight. Thanks

to a strong national following, winning

tradition, and the Cougars’ high-profile

schedule in upcoming years, the national

exposure of BYU Football is sure to

increase. BYU also has four of the top-10

largest audiences in ESPN history.

Few universities have a fan base as loyal,

dedicated and die-hard as BYU. Cougar

fans are some of the greatest fans on

earth. Since 1982 BYU has consistently

ranked among the top five in attendance

in the West (63,333 per game). Thanks

to its affiliation with the LDS Church,

BYU has a strong national following

bringing thousands to away games.

fAciLities

ities, FieldTurf practice field, football hall

of fame, and new locker rooms at both

64,045-seat LaVell Edwards Stadium and

styLe Of pLAy
Cougar football plays the style of foot-

ball that fans love to watch and players

love to play. Wide-open, fast, furious,

high-energy offense and defense are

head coach Bronco Mendenhall: “We will

continue to have an aggressive defense,

along with an up-tempo, high-powered

offense. Our goal is to be as exciting and

competitive as any team in the country.”

field, the Cougars have also excelled. BYU has earned 26 Academic All-

American citations and has had 13 NCAA post-graduate scholars.

STYLE Of pLAY
The Cougars play the style of football that fans love to watch and

players love to play. Wide-open, fast, furious, high-energy offense

and defense are trademarks of BYU football. According to head

coach Bronco Mendenhall: “We will continue to have an aggressive

defense, along with an up-tempo, high-powered offense. Our goal is

to be as exciting and competitive as any team in the country.”

SEASON PREVIEW

aL L   P Re vIe w

Armed with 23 players who have starting experience, includ-

ing eight regular starters on offense, the Cougars are deter-

mined to build on last season’s success. Bronco Mendenhall

returns for his second season after guiding the Cougars to a

second-place finish in the Mountain West Conference and a

trip to the Pioneer PureVision Las Vegas Bowl—the Cougars’

first bowl appearance since 2001. BYU finished the regular

season with a 6-5 record, including a 5-3 mark in league play.

It was BYU’s first non-losing season since the 2001 campaign.

“We expect to be an improved football team, competing at a higher level,” Mendenhall said. “I

expect our football team to perform well in critical situations. I like this football team. I like how

they are working to achieve the standard of excellence in all they do. I like how they’re working.

I like their competitive spirit. I like how they’re competing every day. I like the atmosphere. This

is the best team chemistry I have felt since I began coaching at BYU.”

Mendenhall said the team focus and determination during the off-season has created confidence

heading into Fall Camp.

“The team has been very workman-like during the off-season, just going about their business

with a quiet confidence. I like their confidence.”

Under Mendenhall, and first-year offensive coordinator Robert Anae, the Cougars averaged 33.0

points per contest. BYU racked up over 462 yards per game, including 152.3 yards rushing and

310.1 yards passing per game.

“It was significant for our football program to reach the post-season in 2005,” he added. Looking

to the 2006 season, a lesson can be learned from our experience in 2005.

“We finished second in the Mountain West and lost a hard-fought, tightly contested bowl game. I
have a better idea of what the standard is for this program. Our players have a better idea. We’re

not just satisfied with going to a bowl game. It was a nice step for our program, but we’re not

satisfied to finish second. That wasn’t our goal. There’s a different standard. We’ve seen it, we’ve

CURTIS BROwN

felt it, we’ve touched it and we’ve seen how close it is.”

The Cougars return 13 players with start-

ing experience, including eight regular

starters. The list of returning starters

includes three first-team All-MWC per-

formers in quarterback John Beck, run-

ning back Curtis Brown and tight end

Jonny Harline. Describing his expecta-

tions for the offense in 2006, Mendenhall

said he expects good things to happen.

“The offense is experienced, talented,

deep, confident, stable and productive,”

Mendenhall said. “As a result of these

things, we expect positive things to hap-

pen offensively.

“I was pleased with our offense last
year as we adapted and used person-

nel throughout the season to move the

football and score points. We did an

exceptional job in terms of ball security

and “Blue Zone” scoring. We played good

enough on offense, through the majority

of the year, to give us a chance to win

football games. With the experience,

leadership and depth we have returning,

we expect our offense to be explosive, in

terms of yardage and points.”

2006 BYU FOOTBALL GUIDESEASON PREVIEW

RUNNINg BaCK
Much like the quarterback position, the Cougars have

an experienced player in senior Curtis Brown returning

to carry the load at running back. Last season, Brown

ran for 1,123 yards, fifth most for a BYU back in a

single season, and scored 16 touchdowns (14 rushing,

two receiving), tied for second most in a single season.

Brown was also a threat receiving, finishing the year

with 53 receptions, second on the team, for 454 yards.

KUReSa

Joining the returnees will be McKay Jacobson, a highly

touted recruit from Southlake, Texas out of Southlake

Carroll High School. Jacobson graduated from high

school in December and enrolled at BYU for winter

semester. He has been working out with the team since

arriving in January and participated in Spring Camp.

Other receivers who will look to make an impact include

Brett Cooper, Saia Hafoka, Travis Henderson, Bryce

Mahuika, Sean Mosman, Ryan Neeley, Troy Sheide and

Matt Smith. Mahuika was the team’s primary kick and

punt returner in 2004, but played sparingly last season

due to injury.

Joining Brown will be a crop of talented running backs

who will be looking to make an impact in 2006. Wayne

Latu, Joe Semanoff and Manase Tonga all saw action

in 2005, combining for 132 yards on 23 carries. Ray

Hudson returns after redshirting the 2005 season.

Hudson lettered as a true freshman in 2004.

With Fahu Tahi lost to graduation, the Cougars will look

to returned missionary Fui Vakapuna to replace Tahi’s

quickness and tough, punishing style of play. Vakapuna

lettered as a true freshman in 2003, averaging 5.2 yards

per carry. He was the team’s second leading rusher with

157 yards on 30 carries.

JONNY
haRLINe

The Cougars will also look to incoming freshmen Mike

Hague and Harvey Unga to gain experience and add

depth to the running back position.

“The running back position will be one of our key

strengths on offense,” Mendenhall said. “We’ll have

depth, experience and talent at running back.”

wIDe ReCeIveR
Despite the loss of deep threat Todd Watkins, the

Cougars have several experienced players who are well

suited to be effective in the spread offense. The leading

returning receiver is Nate Meikle, a 2005 second-team

Academic All-American, who caught 36 passes for 292

yards. He is joined by teammates Matt Allen (21 recep-

tions, 272 yards, two touchdowns), Michael Reed (18

receptions, 272 yards, one touchdown) and Zac Collie

(11 receptions, 158 yards, one touchdown).

All four players showcased their talents at different times

during the 2005 season. Meikle was BYU’s leading

receiver in the Las Vegas Bowl as he caught 12 passes

TIghT eND
The tight end position may be one of the deepest on the

Cougar roster. The top returning player is senior Jonny

Harline, who led the team in receptions (63) and receiv-

ing yards (853) and was named first-team All-MWC in

2005. Harline also caught five touchdown passes and

had three 100-yard performances last season, includ-

ing 123 yards against both San Diego State and New

Mexico. Harline returns for his senior season as the

nation’s top returning tight end.

Senior Daniel Coats is also back after a successful 2005

season in which he caught 21 passes for 189 yards

and three touchdowns. Other tight ends who will be in

the mix for playing time include Vic So’oto and Andrew

George.

So’oto showed promise as a pass-catching end in camp

last season and played on special teams throughout the

year.

16

“The tight end position will be another of our offensive

strengths,” Mendenhall said. “We have four quality play-

ers at that position, including Jonny Harline, who returns

as the nation’s top returning tight end. Daniel Coats also

returns after an excellent season in 2005.”

OFFeNSIve LINe
The Cougars will return three of last year’s five starters

on the line, including second-team All-MWC performer

Jake Kuresa, Eddie Keele and freshman All-American

Dallas Reynolds. BYU will have to replace second-team

All-MWC center Lance Reynolds, Jr. and Brian Sanders.

Terence Brown, a regular contributor last season, has

announced his intentions to serve a mission.

The line helped the BYU offense put up big numbers

in 2005, including 33.0 points and 462.4 total yards

per game. Despite the losses, there are several players

who are capable of picking up the slack. Ray Feinga

saw action in 2005 and will be expected to battle for a

eDDIe KeeLe

Bright, who was expected to contribute

last season but, suffered an season-

is one of three players who will be battling

for the starting position at center. Erik

Freeman and Sete Aulai will also compete

at center.

Other players who will be competing for

time on the line during Fall Camp include

Nick Longshore, David Oswald and Marco

Thorson.

“While we return three starters, we’ve

lost some experience,” Mendenhall said.

“However, I feel very good about our top-

eight guys.

sive line. As a result, our spring practice

format will be unique. Through recruiting,

however, we’ve been able to address the

depth issue along the offensive line.”

When Fall Camp begins, the Cougars will welcome Tom

Sorensen, a transfer from Vanderbilt University, as well

as Rick Wolfley, a first-team all-state selection from Star

Valley, Wyoming. Kamehameha High School standout

Walter Kahaialii will also journey to Provo this fall. Two

other incoming linemen include 2005 Sports Illustrated

Preseaon High School All-American Braden Hansen and
all-state lineman Ryan Freeman.

DeFeNSIve OveRvIew
Following an off-season review of the program,

Jaime Hill joins the staff after three years as an assistant

coach in the Canadian Football League, including the

past two seasons as the co-defensive coordinator for the

Ottawa Renegades. Hill brings over 20 years of coaching

experience to the BYU coaching staff. He will be respon-

sible for coaching the defensive secondary, including

corners and safeties. Long-time coach, Barry Lamb, will
coach the outside linebackers and Paul Tidwell will be

responsible for the inside linebackers. Steve Kaufusi will

continue coaching the defensive line and Mendenhall

will continue his responsibilities as the defensive coor-

Mendenhall and the defensive coaching staff have imple-

mented a handful of changes heading into Spring Camp.

dinator.

ments around to make sure we get our best 11 players

on the field.”

With only four defensive starters returning, Mendenhall

said his goal this spring is to find players who are ready

to contribute based on effort, execution and discipline.

“Defensively, we have a lot of potential,” he said. “I like

our personnel. I really like this group of players and how

hard they’ve worked.”

replacing the entire starting front line in 2006.

Daniel Marquardt, who all started at least six

games in 2005, have departed due to gradua-

tion.

from last season,” Mendenhall said. “Like the

Despite the loss of two starters, the linebacker

position will be the key strength for BYU.

According to Mendenhall, the linebacker position

will be the featured position in 2006.

“The linebacking corps is the strength of our

defense,” Mendenhall said. “Because of the num-

bers and ability at that position, our core defen-

sive package for the 2006 season will include

more linebackers.”

Cameron Jensen, a second-team All-MWC per-

former and a team captain, will play middle line-

backer and will be joined by several talented

players who saw action in 2005. Jensen led

the team in tackles for the second-consec-

utive season with 84, including 44 solo
takedowns.

Markell Staffieri, who played in every game

last season and made two starts, will be a

favorite to start alongside Jensen. Staffieri,

Matt allen
Peoria, Ariz. (Cactus HS)

Junior, 5-11, 180

Wide Receiver

at BYU: 2005: (Sophomore) Earned letterman honors ... named Academic All-MWC ...
saw action in all 12 games of the season ... credited with 21 receptions, 272 receiv-
ing yards and two touchdowns on the season ... averaged 13 yards per reception ...
recorded one solo tackle and one assisted tackle on the season ... longest reception
was a 28-yard touchdown against Utah ... had three receptions for 39 yards against
Utah ... had a season-high 48 receiving yards against Eastern Illinois ... caught the
game-winning touchdown against New Mexico, a 23-yarder with 1:40 left in the
fourth ... had two receptions for 40 yards against the Lobos. 2004: (Freshman) Earned
letterman honors as a redshirt freshman ... saw action in six games throughout the
season ... credited with one reception for 26 yards in the Cougars’ 49-16 win against
San Diego State ... had one kick return for 25 yards against UNLV ... credited with six
tackles on special teams ... also participated in spring drills. 2003: (Redshirt) Named
Prep Offensive Player of the week against SDSU.

HIGH SCHOOl: A three-year letterwinner at Cactus High School ... recorded 53 recep-
tions for 1,011 yards and 13 touchdowns as a sophomore ... earned first-team
all-state and all-region honors as a sophomore ... missed the first eight games of the
season as a junior after having wrist surgery prior to the season ... as a senior, cred-
ited with 57 receptions for 992 yards ... produced 22 total touchdowns ... earned
All-Arizona honorable mention honors ... named first-team all-region ... helped lead
the Cobras to a 34-4 record in three seasons at Cactus High School ... a four-year let-

record in Western States Conference Northern Division play ... tied as the top-ranked

college ranking ... earned unanimous All-Western States Conference first-team honors
in 2003 ... threw for 2,052 yards as a sophomore, including 1,681 yards during the
regular season ... completed 179 of 231 attempts for a 77.5 completion percent-
age with only three interceptions ... credited with 12 of the Cougars’ 17 touchdown
completions on the season ... ranked third on the Canyons’ roster with 36 points,

ing up 430 yards on 107 carries during the season ... averaged 4.02 yards per carry

in the Cougars’ win over Ventura — the team’s eighth straight win of the season ... a
Junior College Academic All-American.

wife, Jamie, played soccer at BYU ... parents are John and Mary Beck.

John Beck

2004: (Sophomore) Earned second-team All-Mountain West honors as a sophomore
... started at quarterback in 10 of the Cougars’ 11 games during the season ... broke

league opponents ... ranked second in the MWC, averaging 233.0 yards per game
(vs. all opponents) ... named the MWC Offensive Player of the Week after leading

the Cougars’ 49-16 win over San Diego State, marking the highest quarterback rat-
ing since early in the 2001 season ... passed the 3,000-yard mark as a sophomore,
ranking as the Cougars’ third most prolific sophomore quarterback ... had at least
one 50+ yard completion in six of 11 games ... connected on a season-high 82-yard
touchdown completion (to Austin Collie) vs. SDSU ... recorded a season-high 390
yards on 35 attempts against Boise State ... ranked second in the MWC with 15
touchdown completions on the season ... recorded a season-high four touchdown

2003: (Freshman) Earned Academic All-Mountain West honors ... also earned letter-
man honors as a true freshman ... competed in eight games, including four starts
... became only the second true freshman to start at quarterback in BYU history (vs.

Cardinal, including a season-long 56-yard bomb to Rodney Wilkerson ... completed
19-of-35 attempts for 193 yards and one touchdown against Air Force ... credited
with his first career victory on Oct. 4, leading the Cougars to a 44-36 win at San
Diego State ... credited with 17-of-26 attempts for 180 yards and a season-high
two touchdowns ... completed a 35-yard lob to Fahu Tahi to tie the score at 14-14
against the Aztecs ... after throwing two interceptions in his first game as a starter (vs.
Stanford), did not throw a single interception in over 80 attempts, spanning a stretch
of two straight games ... saw action at fourth-ranked USC, completing one pass for
12 yards ... carried the ball four times for 29 yards ... suffered a concussion against
the Trojans ... saw his first collegiate action in the first half of the Cougars’ 24-13
win over Georgia Tech ... sacked for a 10-yard loss on his first play from scrimmage
... was credited with a fumble (recovered by BYU) on his second play from scrim-
mage ... threw an interception on his first collegiate pass attempt -- his third play
from scrimmage ... participated in spring drills after returning from a Church mission
... completed a combined 20-of-39 attempts for 260 yards in three different spring
scrimmages ... completed 6-of-6 attempts for 157 yards and three touchdowns in the
Cougars’ second spring scrimmage.

pionship as a senior ... posted an overall record of 25-2 over his junior and senior
seasons ... earned first-team all-region, first-team all-state, USA Today All-American
honorable mention and Dairyman first-team All-America honors ... named the region
Offensive Player of the Year ... garnered first-team Super All-State and Super All-
State Player of the Year honors ... recognized as the Arizona High School Football
Player of the Year ... earned both the Ed Dougherty and Fred Enke Awards (Arizona
Quarterback of the Year) ... named the MVP during the state championship game as
a senior ... selected as the Arizona All-Star game Most Valuable Player ... recognized
by Fox Sports as the Arizona 5A Player of the Year ... holds the Arizona high school
record with 42 touchdown completions in a single season with only 4 interceptions ...
also lettered twice in baseball as a pitcher and an infielder.

ried his wife Barbara of Ferron, Utah in 2004.

Graduate  Ass is tant,   Defense

DUaNe BUSBY
Di re ctor  of  Football  Operations

Micah Alba, raised in Marietta, Ga., is entering his
first season as a graduate assistant. He was a three-
year letterwinner at cornerback for BYU and as a
senior, led the Cougars in interceptions. Alba gradu-
ated from BYU with a major in history and a minor
in Spanish. During the 2005 season, he served as a
volunteer assistant coach working with the defense.
He will be pursuing a Masters degree in Exercise Science Physiology. Micah and
his wife Wendy are the proud parents of two children.

STeve CLaRK
Graduate  Ass is tant,   Offense

A native of Provo, Steve Clark is in his first season with
the Cougars as a graduate assistant. Clark returns to his
alma mater after serving as the quarterback coach and
passing game coordinator at St. Mary’s College in Calif.
Before coming to BYU, Clark was the director of football
operations at the University of Utah (2002). In 2001 he
was the defensive intern at Utah. From 1994-2000 Clark

served as an assistant football coach at Provo High School. He and his wife Suzanne
are the parents of four children, Alex, Jamison, Emily and Maggie.

MICK hILL
Footb all  Equipment  Manager

Mick  Hill,  a  native  of  Corvallis,  Ore.,  has  been  with  the
department  since  1981.  He  attended  Orem  High  and
went  on  to  graduate  from  BYU  with  degrees  in  business
and  physical  education.  His  primary  responsibilities  are
with  football,  although  he  manages  the  equipment  for
the  baseball  team  as  well.  He  serves  on  a  number  of
advisory  boards  including  Nike.  He  is  a  certified  equip-
ment  manager  through  the  A.E.M.A.  and  is  married  to  Melanie  Goodrich.  They  have
two daughters who are married and two sons — one serving a mission and the other
an active member of the USMC.

Vid eo   Co ordinato r

Keith Kearl became a part of the athletic program in

tor.  Coming from a background of software testing, video
training and sales with a mechanical engineering firm and
a spinoff software company, he is particularly prepared for
his responsibilities here, including acquisition, editing and
production of videos for game preparation and review. A

local product, Kearl attended both Provo High School and Timpview High School before
going on to UVSC and BYU. He served a mission in Columbus, Ohio.

Amy was hired as the secretary to Bronco Mendenhall
in July 2006. Holker graduated from Timpanogos High
School in Orem, Utah. She worked for a year as a stu-
dent secretary prior to being hired as the Head Coach
Secretary. She is the daughter of Jack and Nancy Holker.

Duane  Busby  joined  the  football  program  in  1996  and
served as an administrative assistant to coach Edwards for
five years. In 2001 he was named the director of football
operations at BYU. He was born and raised in Arizona and
attended  Northern  Arizona  University  and  later  received
a  B.A.  in  English  in  1984  from  Arizona  State.  He  spent
five years as the equipment manager and video coordina-
tor  at  Ricks  College  in  Rexburg,  Idaho.  Busby  taught  at
Madison  High  in  Rexburg,  where  he  was  a  football  and  basketball  assistant  coach.
Busby was an assistant to Arizona governor Bruce Babbitt and was managing editor of
The Wickenburg Sun.

JeFF ReYNOLDS
Football   Me dia  Relations

A native of Castle Rock, Wash., Jeff Reynolds joined
the BYU athletic department in 1999 as the assistant
director of athletic media relations. Reynolds graduated
from Utah State in 1996 with a degree in journalism.
Prior to joining the BYU staff, Reynolds was the assistant
commissioner at the Mid-Continent Conference, and has
also worked at Western Kentucky University and the Sun
Belt Conference. Reynolds has been an active member

of CoSIDA since 1995, serving on several committees. He is also a member of the
National Football Writers Association, Track & Field Writers Association and has won
numerous citations for his part in helping to produce the BYU football and men’s
volleyball media guides. Reynolds is married to the former Char’Ree Brockbank. The
couple has six children: Britneylee, Reece, Alexandria, twins Claira and Evellyn, and
Georiga.

KevIN MORRIS
Head  Foo tbal l  Trai ner

Kevin Morris is in his seventh year as an athletic trainer
at BYU. In 1997 Morris returned to BYU for graduate
work and served as a graduate assistant athletic trainer
while working on his Master of Science in athletic training.
From 1999 to 2000 he was also involved as the head
athletic trainer at Payson High School and clinical trainer
at Sports and Physical Therapy in Payson. Morris became
an assistant athletic trainer and received his Masters in
Science degree from BYU in 2003. He was named the head football trainer in 2004.
He served two terms as vice president of the Utah Athletic Trainers Association and was
awarded the G.E. Moose Detty Scholarship Award from the National Athletic Trainers
Association for outstanding academic achievement in athletic training. Morris, a native of
Aztec, N.M., is married to the former Lynn Knowles. They have three children, Megan,
Zackery and Benjamin.

ShIRLeY JOhNSON
Football   Secretar y

A native of Salt Lake City, Johnson has served in the
BYU football office for 26 years under three head
coaches. After graduating from BYU in English and
teaching for a short time, she returned to BYU to work
with the graduate students in Educational Psychology.
She was there for eight years before moving to football.
She was the recipient of a President’s Appreciation
Award in 1990.

COUGARS ON MISSIONS

One of the first questions Bronco Mendenhall was asked
after becoming the head coach at BYU was what he
thought about the missionary program, and if he felt it
helped or hurt the BYU football program.

between the ages of 19 and 26. A substantial number of
young women and older couples also serve proselytizing
missions.

than 160 nations and territories. With over 12.2 million
members, it is one of the fastest growing religions in the
world, and one of the largest Christian churches in the
United States.

“Serving missions is what makes this team, this univer-
sity and the church unique,” Mendenhall said. “These
unique characteristics are based upon sound principles
and values. Why would we not embrace them?”

Of the 105 student-athletes slated to participate in
fall camp, 62 (59 percent) have served full-time, two-
year missions in a combined total of over 21 different
countries around the world, including Australia, Brazil,
Canada, Chile, Ecuador, England, Germany, Guatemala,
Norway, Philippines, Portugal, Russia, Samoa and
Tonga—just to name a few.

Brigham Young University is owned and operated by The
Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints. The mission-
ary emphasis of the Church is perhaps one of its most
recognized characteristics.

The Church operates more than 330 missions around
the world. A mission covers a geographic area and has a
central headquarters. Each is presided over by a mission
president who is called from the ranks of the Church
membership to serve for a period of three years. The
mission president directs the work of the missionaries
assigned to his mission.

Approximately 52,000 missionaries, representing the
Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints, are serv-
ing proselytizing missions in 330 missions around the
world. Seventy-five percent of the Church’s
proselytizing missionaries are young men

Missionaries work long hours—seven days a week for
two years, or 18 months for women and couples—
teaching the Gospel of Jesus Christ and participating in
community service.

In addition, about 5,100 individuals (including couples)
are given special service assignments. Health special-
ists and doctors go to developing countries where the
Church’s health services program teaches preventive
care. Craftsmen, artisans and construction supervisors
train members in local building projects. Agricultural
experts train people to produce food more effectively and
economically. Other mission assignments include educa-
tion, family history research and leadership training.

 The missionaries and/or their families donate money to
the Church to pay for their personal expenses. When his
or her assignment is completed, the missionary returns
home to pursue vocational, academic or other personal
goals.

Aside from their brief orientation at a missionary train-
ing center, missionaries receive little formal training for
their ministry. Missionary preparation comes primarily
from personal study and, in many cases, from examples
taught in the home from childhood.

The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints was
officially organized on April 6, 1830 with six members.
Today, congregations of the Church are found in more

The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints is
Christian, but is neither Catholic nor Protestant. Rather,
it is a restoration of the original church established by
Jesus Christ.

eQUIPMeNT aND LOCKeR ROOMS
The 75-yard-long locker room is patterned after the NFL’s

Philadelphia Eagles. There is also a special locker room for former

BYU players who want to train in Provo during the off season.

TRaININg FaCILITIeS
Sponsored by Dynatronics, this area features a rehabilitation room

and a hydro area with a cold pool, a hot tub with power jets, a

treadmill pool with two cameras and a doctor’s office. Trainers

provide electrical stimulation and ultra sound treatments as well

as bikes, treadmills and Cybex machines.

STReNgTh aND CONDITIONINg CeNTeR
Most of the Strength and Conditioning Center’s equipment was

custom built by Free Motion and is sponsored by Icon Heath

and Fitness. Among the features are 12 full power racks with

platforms, seven half racks, three jerk racks, Sorinex hurricane

racks, reverse hypers, Agaton Fitness Systems, an outdoor mondo

plyometric warmup area, two super leg drive sleds, two different

dumbbell areas and a medicine ball wall. The first floor also has

a nutrition area sponsored by Pharmanex. The second floor is the

speed development area has six Tomahawk spinning bikes and

TVs, five Nordic Track incline trainers, three Versaclimbers, two

Scifit upper body bikes, Woodway force treadmills, H/P Cosmos

treadmills with Pneu Weight devices, vibratory boards, a step mill,

a turfed agility drill area with resistance and a testing area.

sports psychologist and a nutritionist.

INDOOR PRaCTICe FaCILITY
 The IPF is 422 feet wide and 222 feet deep with a height
of 86 feet at the center (106,000 square feet). Among its
features are two artificial turf fields (55 yards & 65 yards),
retractable goalposts on each end, a sideline staging facil-
ity for offensive linemen and four batting cages. The indoor
surface is field turf and the building features four video
platforms located 40 feet above the playing surface. The
IPF serves as an indoor practice arena for the football, soc-
cer, golf, baseball and softball teams and a variety of other
sports programs, including extramurals and intramurals.

every year. Some of the stadium’s well-known fea-

grass playing surface and a four-level press box.

BYU TRADITIONS

Y MOUNTaIN

Preparations for placing the initials BYU on the moun-
tainside began in April of 1906 when president George
H. Brimhall commissioned surveyors for the letters “B,”
“Y” and “U.” The letter “Y” was first laid out to ensure
that the initials were properly centered on the mountain.
Beginning in the early morning, students formed a line
that zigzagged up the mountain. Standing eight feet
apart, each student would carry a load up to the next
and then return for another.

The project, which many expected to be com-
pleted by 10 a.m., lasted until 4 p.m. and only the “Y”
was covered. After such a laborious day of work, plans
to fill in the other two letters were scrapped. Covered
with a thin layer of lime powder, the letter was in need
of constant repair. Hoping to make it more permanent, a
layer of rock was added in 1907. The next year, 20,000
pounds of sand and cement were carried up the moun-
tain to form a three-foot high wall around the letter to
hold it together. Several years later the letter underwent
a facelift as the blocks, or serifs, were added to it to give
it its current appearance.

A nationally recognized symbol of BYU, Y

Mountain is a featured shot of almost every Cougar
game broadcast on television. Located about half a mile
east of campus and halfway up the mountain, the “Y”
looks out over the valley and is one of its most promi-
nent features.

Collegefootballnews.com Oct. 15, 2003

on nearby farms. Both were recaptured later the same
day. Three weeks later, one cougar died and the other
was taken to the old Salt Lake Zoo. BYU procured its
mascots from nearby zoos and local bounty hunters but
never owned its own cougars again.

Cosmo the Cougar, the brainchild of pep chairman

Dwayne Stevenson, made his first official appearance
before BYU fans on October 15, 1953. At a cost of 73
dollars for the costume, Daniel T. Gallego, Stevenson’s
roommate, became the first man under the fur. The
name given the big cat was derived from BYU’s diverse
student body.

“BYU was a very international, cosmopolitan
school and that is where we got the name Cosmo,” said
Gallego.

With over a half-century of service to the BYU
community, Cosmo has become an icon of Cougar sports
to fans and foes across the country. Through Cosmo’s
Kids Club and Cosmo’s Corner, he is helping raise another
generation of BYU fans. Prowling the sidelines for the
Cougars, his antics add to the experience of game day at
BYU. Cosmo brings out the best of Cougar spirit in fans at
athletic competitions both home and away.

Y Mountain: directly east of BYU campus

COSMO

The cougar, chosen as BYU’s mascot by former coach
Eugene L. Roberts in the early 1920s, has undergone
a tremendous evolution since its inception to become
what is now known as the feline phenomenon of Cosmo
the Cougar. Beginning in 1924 when BYU purchased a
pair of cougar cubs for 50 cents each to excite BYU fans
at athletic events, live cougars prowled the sidelines of
BYU games on a regular basis through the late 40s and
on special occasions through the 60s.

The original cubs were housed on the south side
of campus until 1929 when they managed to break out

of their cage, kill two dogs and began stalking livestock

Cosmo helps lead cheers at Edwards Stadium

88

 The BeehIve BOOT

First instituted in 1971, the Beehive Boot is the symbol
of gridiron supremacy in the state of Utah. The idea for
the rotating trophy originated with Dave Schulthess, for-
mer Sports Information Director (SID) of BYU, and was
agreed upon by SIDs from Utah, Utah State and Weber
State. As the trophy would represent dominance within
the confines of the Beehive State, it was decided that
the trophy should be symbolic of the people Utah was
built by—the Mormon pioneers.

Finding a suitable pioneer boot for use as a trophy

proved to be a difficult task. As pioneers wore their
boots until there simply wasn’t much left of them, there
weren’t many pioneer boots lying around. After an ardu-
ous search that included numerous personal calls and

The Beehive Boot has been awarded to the Cougars 19
times since 1971.

radio announcements, Tom Beasley, an antique dealer
living in Cache Valley donated a pair of boots for use as
the trophy. Estimated to be 100 or more years old, the
Beehive Boot is an authentic piece of Utah History.

Awarded to the team with the best record against
in-state opponents, the Beehive Boot lived its first four
years at Utah State University. BYU then began a domi-
nant run as it ascended into the realms of football’s
elite, claiming the Boot 19 times in the past 30 years.
Utah has won the boot nine times. The longest streak in
the history of the Beehive Boot is a five-year span from
1983 to 1987 by BYU. After a two-year stint in Provo,
the Boot currently resides in Salt Lake. The Cougars look
to bring it back home this season.

If Utah’s teams should end the season with iden-

tical records against in-state opponents, a winner is
chosen by vote of the in-state media who covered the
schools’ games. This situation has occurred twice in the
history of the boot (1973 and 1997) and Utah State
was given the trophy each time.

2006 BYU FOOTBALL GUIDE

BYU TRADITIONS

COUgaR FIghT SONg

The vICTORY BeLL

Penned in 1932 by Clyde D. Sandgren, “The Cougar Song” is the anthem of
BYU sports. Sung after each touchdown and field goal at football games, its words
can frequently be heard ringing out across Utah Valley on Saturday afternoons in the
fall. Traditionally, fans remain seated through the verse and then stand, chant and
shout through the chorus. Carried by 64,000 voices “The Cougar Song” can be an
intimidating musical number to an opposing team as LaVell Edwards Stadium thun-
ders to its tune.

Although written and composed in 1932, the song was not copyrighted and pre-
sented to the University until 1947. In addition to “The Cougar Song” Sandgren wrote
and composed “The Old ‘Y’ Bell” which was sung at the dedication of the upper-cam-
pus bell tower that housed the Victory Bell before it was moved its current location
outside the Marriott Center.

A 1937 graduate of BYU, Sandgren went on to obtain a law degree from St.

John’s University. He then became a partner of the New York law firm Burroughs and
Brown until 1947 when he decided to return to his native Provo to establish his own
law practice. Sandgren later served as general counsel and vice president of BYU.
Although he passed away in 1989, Sandgren’s memory lives on through the voices of
Cougar fans across the nation.

The Cougar Song
By Clyde D. Sandgren,1932

Rise all loyal Cougars and hurl your challenge to the foe.
You will fight, day or night, rain or snow.
Loyal, strong, and true
Wear the white and blue.
While we sing, get set to spring.
Come on Cougars it’s up to you. Oh!

Rise and shout, the Cougars are out
along the trail to fame and glory.
Rise and shout, our cheers will ring out
As you unfold your victr’y story.

On we go, to vanquish the foe for Alma Mater’s sons and daughters.
As we join in song, in praise of you, our faith is strong,
We’ll raise our colors high in the blue,
And cheer our Cougars of BYU.

The original Y bell traveled across the plains with the early pioneers of the
Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints and was used to call meetings among
the saints and to announce any deaths in the
community. Donated to the Brigham Young
Academy in 1875, it was used to begin and
dismiss classes until it was destroyed in a fire
in 1884. The Academy went without a bell for
the next 28 years, using instead a variety of
instruments as a class bell.

Rung after each BYU victory, the bell
was quieted in 1949 after a basketball win
over rival Utah. Several overzealous students
climbed the tower and vigorously beat upon
the bell until it cracked. Repaired with a bronze
patch in 1954 by Frank Hemingway, the bell
was removed from the education building and
mounted on a trailer so it could travel with BYU’s teams to away games.

Its mobility proved its downfall as it was stolen in 1958 to be found six months

later in nearby Springville. The theft prompted the construction of a bell tower on
BYU’s upper-campus overlooking the Smith Fieldhouse. Dedicated in 1959, the
tower served well until 1973 when the bell fell while ringing in the dedication of
the Marriott Center. The supporting yoke above the bell was broken and the bell
shattered. The bell was soon repaired by Ray Mortenson of the BYU Maintenance
shop using electric welding—miraculously maintaining its previous tone—and was
remounted in the tower.

In 1978, the bell was moved to its current home on the southwest corner of
the Marriott Center. The open arch tower was designed by BYU landscape architect
Boyd Dawler and is made of the same cast stone as the exterior pillars of the Marriott
Center. Although the old bell tower above the Smith Fieldhouse no longer stands, the
massive steel I-beams that supported the tower have now been converted into the
strongest park benches in the Intermountain West.

Over the years the ringing of the bell has come to represent honor, achievement

and spirit, and has inspired several poems and songs from BYU faculty and alumni.
Clyde D. Sandgren, who composed the Cougar Fight Song, penned a verse that was
sung at the dedication of the upper-campus bell tower. As BYU victories have become
more and more commonplace in recent decades, the ringing of the “Y bell” after each
win has renamed it the “Victory Bell.”

2006 BYU FOOTBALL GUIDE

STRENGTH & CONDITIONING

C onditioning encompasses all facets of

physical performance. From speed and

quickness, to cardiovascular endurance and

brute strength, BYU’s strength and conditioning head

coach Jay Omer creates programs designed to increase

them all. Training programs are sport-specific and posi-

tion-specific within each sport, allowing athletes to focus

“I like to see kids work hard, make something

of themselves and improve their abilities. That’s

the joy of this job. I’m also on the same page

with coach Mendenhall. We believe a team can

on what they need to succeed in their individual com-

only be successful if it prepares well and has

petitive endeavors.

In no sport is the need for strength more evident

than in football. Each game, each play, can be decided

by who tires first. Week in and week out, players give

team chemistry. You can have a team of super

stars, but I’d rather have a team of less talent

and take a beating and are expected to come back for

that works hard, cares about each other, has

more of the same the next week. Less than a year after

his arrival in 2001, Omer’s subjects were seeing huge

returns on their investment. Most players had increased

their personal lifts 15-25 percent. Mike Tanner, a line-

team chemistry and will go to war because they

believe in each other. That’s what it’s all about

backer who was bench-pressing 330 pounds when

and that’s what we’re trying to get done here.”

Omer arrived, was lifting 400 at summer’s end.

    Conditioning also increases the production of ath-

letes as it helps to prevent injury. Although football

is a contact sport where injuries are common, proper

conditioning can help to avoid major injuries and reduce

the recovery time of minor injuries. An athlete in top

condition tends to recover faster and be better able to

withstand the rigors of physical activity.

  Not only does the conditioning program improve

each player as an individual, but it also improves the

team as a whole. Working and sweating with each other

in the weight room helps bring the players together.

Seeing each person sacrifice and succeed in the off-sea-

son builds a camaraderie that carries over to the field as

players work to make each other and the team better.

“He (Omer) has brought us a feeling of team chem-

istry, working out together and being accountable,” said

Teag Whiting, a former offensive lineman. “There’s noth-

ing we’re going to see on the football field this season

that’s any tougher or harder than we’re facing in our

workouts. It makes us close.”

The results BYU players have realized have come

from dedication and sacrifice. It’s a time-consuming and

often painful process in which athletes push themselves

to higher levels of performance, and Omer is there to

watch over them the whole time.

“The man is a sadist,” said former offensive guard

Jason Scukanec, “[but] you find yourself doing it. You

find yourself pushing yourself and you don’t know why.

You don’t want to disappoint him because it means

something to you and your teammates.”

Each fall when the Cougars take the field, they carry

with them the hopes of BYU fans across the nation. With

each passing week and after every win, those hopes get

bigger and weigh more on the players.  Arriving at the

gym before most people are out of bed and staying until

long after most people have gone home for the night,

Jay Omer takes those hopes very seriously.  It’s his job

to make sure the players are strong enough to carry

those hopes to one more victory, one more title, one

more bowl.

 — Jay Omer, BYU Head Strength & Conditioning Coach

University President

 Cecil O. Samuelson began his work as the 12th presi-

dent of Brigham Young University on May 1, 2003.

 Dr. Samuelson is a Salt Lake City native who served

at the University of Utah as professor of medicine,

dean of the School of Medicine and vice president of

health sciences. Prior to his call as a full-time General

Authority of The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day

TOM hOLMOe
Director of Athletics

On March 1, 2005, President Cecil O. Samuelson

announced the appointment of Tom Holmoe as

Director of Athletics at Brigham Young University.

Holmoe is the 12th athletics director in school history

and the first to oversee both the men’s and women’s

sports programs.

A former Cougar defensive back from 1978-82,

Saints, he was senior vice president of Intermountain Health Care. He holds a bach-

Holmoe returned to BYU in July 2001 as Associate Athletic Director of Development.

K. FReD SKOUSeN
Advancement Vice President

Dr. K. Fred Skousen is in his sixth year as advancement

vice president of Brigham Young University. Previously,

he was Dean of the Marriott School of Management

and Director of the School of Accountancy at BYU. He

earned a Bachelor’s degree from BYU and Masters and

Ph.D. degrees from the University of Illinois.

Dr. Skousen has been a consultant to the Financial

Executive Research Foundation, the Controller General of the United States, the

Federal Trade Commission and to several large companies. During the summer

of 1969, he was a Faculty Resident on the staff of the Securities and Exchange

Commission in Washington, D.C. During the summer of 1973, he was a Faculty

Fellow with Price Waterhouse & Co., in Los Angeles. Dr. Skousen currently serves on

the board of directors of several corporations.

Dr. Skousen taught at the University of Illinois and the University of Minnesota prior

to joining the faculty at BYU. Professor Skousen was appointed to a nine-member

National Commission on Professional Accounting Education in 1982. Dr. Skousen

held the J. Willard and Alice S. Marriott Chair of Management at BYU from 1989-

1998. Dr. Skousen is the author or co-author of more than 50 articles, research

reports and books, including: “An Introduction to the SEC,” “Intermediate Accounting,”

“Accounting: Concepts and Applications,” and “Financial Accounting.” Fred and his

wife, Julie, have five sons, one daughter and 20 grandchildren.

92

After retiring from pro football, Holmoe entered the coaching ranks, returning to

BYU to serve as a graduate assistant under LaVell Edwards in 1990 and 1991.

Holmoe then accepted an offer from Bill Walsh to become the Stanford secondary

coach in 1992, where he remained for two seasons. The Cardinal was PAC-10 tri-

champions in 1992 with a 10-3 overall record, including a win over Penn State in

the Blockbuster Bowl. In 1994, Holmoe returned to the 49ers, serving as George

Seifert’s defensive backfield coach for two seasons. Two years later in 1996, Holmoe

joined the University of

California staff as defen-

sive coordinator under

Steve Mariucci. Following

Mariucci’s departure

to the NFL, Holmoe

assumed duties as head

coach of the Golden

Bears’ football program

from 1997 until resigning

after the 2001 season.

Holmoe graduated from

BYU with a degree in

zoology in 1983 and

received a Master’s

degree from BYU in

athletic administration in

1995. He and his wife,

Lori, live in Provo with

their four children.

a LOOK aT The 2006 SCheDULe
The Mountain West Conference 2006 football schedule is comprised of 72

games, including 36 conference match-ups. The Mountain West will begin its

eighth season of football competition Labor Day weekend with an eight-game

schedule. San Diego State opens the 2006 campaign hosting UTEP on

Thursday, August 31.  Conference play begins September 23 with San Diego

State hosting Utah and Air Force visiting Wyoming. Eighteen of the league’s

35 non-conference games are against teams that earned bowl berths in 2005

including Boise State (twice), Boston College, Colorado, Fresno State, Iowa

State, Missouri, Nevada (twice), Navy, Notre Dame, Texas Tech, Tulsa,

UCLA, UTEP (twice), Virginia and Wisconsin. Below is a list of non-league

opponents by conference.

ACC: Boston College, Virginia
Big 12: Baylor, Colorado, Iowa State, Missouri, Texas Tech
Big East: Syracuse
Big Ten: Wisconsin
C-USA: UTEP (2), Tulsa
Pac-10: Arizona, UCLA
SEC: Tennessee
WAC: Boise State (2), Fresno State, Hawai`i, New Mexico State,
Nevada (2), San Jose State, Utah State (3)
Independent: Army, Navy, Notre Dame
Non-DI: Cal Poly, Idaho State, Northern Arizona, Portland State,
Weber State, UC Davis

The Mountain West Conference

is made up of nine Universities

from seven Western states. TCU

was added to the conference

on 2005.

MwC COaCheS
Air Force head coach Fisher DeBerrycarries the torch in terms of longevity in

the Mountain West Conference. DeBerry has coached 21 seasons at the

Academy, followed by Colorado State’s Sonny Lubickwith 12 seasons under

his belt in Fort Collins. Rocky Longof New Mexico is third on the list as he

enters the ninth season at the helm of his alma mater. TCU head coach Gary

Patterson enters his seventh year, while Wyoming’s Joe Glennenters his

fourth campaign in Laramie.  Bronco Mendenhall of BYU, Mike Sanfordof

UNLV and Utah’s Kyle Whittingham are each entering their second seasons

at their respective institutions. The Mountain West will welcome one new head coach

in 2006, San Diego State’s Chuck Long who takes over for the Aztecs after serving as

the Offensive Coordinator for Oklahoma from 2002-05.

“Entertainment  Capital  of  the  World”  Las  Vegas  on
December 22 at Sam Boyd Stadium. Last year, BYU nearly
pulled off an amazing fourth-quarter comeback against Cal
in the 14th annual Las Vegas Bowl. The BYU vs. Cal game
also set an attendance record for the up-and-coming bowl
game.  The  Mountain  West  Conference  has  a  4-3  record
against the PAC-10 in the Las Vegas Bowl.

POINSeTTIa BOwL
The San Diego County Credit Union Poinsettia Bowl, in its
second year, will match a team from the Mountain West
Conference against an at-large opponent. A 7 p.m. kickoff
is set for this year’s game that will be played on Tuesday,
December 19 at Qualcomm Stadium. The Poinsettia Bowl
was  established  by  the  organizers  of  the  Holiday  Bowl
and they get the No. 2 choice of Mountain West Teams.

FORT wORTh BOwL
The PlainsCapital Fort Worth Bowl signed a bowl agree-
ment with the MWC that runs through the 2009 football
campaign. It not only assures the Fort Worth Bowl access
to one of the top three selections from the Mountain West,
it  also  allows  access  to  TCU’s  participation  in  the  game
and the possible selection of the MWC football champion
in  any  given  year.  The  2006  Fort  Worth  Bowl  matches
a  team  from  Conference  USA  against  an  opponent  from
the MWC.

New MexICO BOwL
Owned by ESPN, the newly created college football bowl
game  will  be  played  at  the  University  of  New  Mexico’s
University  Stadium  in  Albuquerque,  New  Mexico.  The
game  will  match  a  team  from  the  Mountain  West
Conference  against  a  team  from  the  Western  Athletic
Conference  on  Saturday,  December  23  and  will  be  tele-
vised by ESPN.

2006 BOwL ChaMPIONShIP SeRIeS
January 1 – Fiesta Bowl
January 1 – Rose Bowl
January 2 – Orange Bowl
January 3 – Sugar Bowl
January 8 – BCS National Championship (Phoenix)

The MwC aND The BCS
With  the  addition  of  a  fifth  BCS  bowl  game  in  2006,  Bowl
Championship  Series  officials  announced  modifications  to  the
at-large qualification requirements. Instead of having to finish in
the top 12 of the final BCS standings, teams finishing in the top
14 will now be eligible for a BCS game.
• Any Division I-A team is eligible for at-large consideration.
•  Automatic qualifiers: No. 1 and No. 2 teams in the final BCS
standings.  Big  East,  Big  Ten,  SEC,  Pac10,  Big  12  and  ACC
champions and Notre Dame (if it finishes in the top eight).
•  A non-automatic qualifying BCS team that finishes in the top
12, or in the top 16 if they’re ahead of a BCS champion.

2006 BYU FOOTBALL GUIDE

THE RIVALRY

vs.

The BYU vs. Utah rivalry has become one of the most-heated and most-
anticipated match-ups in the nation. The Utes and the Cougars have met
79 times since 1922. Utah dominated the rivalry until 1965 when the
Cougars began to hold the advantage over the Utes. Since 1965 BYU is
26-14 against the Utes. Except for 2004, the last seven meetings in this
rivalry have been decided by seven points or less including two last-minute
comebacks by BYU in 2000 and 2001.

“I think people from the South and from the Midwest ought

to see this. This is a big-time football rivalry.”

    – Bill Curry, ESPN College Football Analyst

It all started on October 14, 1922.  Well, according to
BYU, anyway. Utah claims the first meeting between
the schools was on April 6, 1896, when BYU was still
Brigham Young Academy.

It’s just one of many things the two schools don’t

agree on. The Academy played Utah six times between
1896 and 1898, before Provo could officially boast its
own full-fledged university. Each team won three games.
The Cougars and the Utes have built a strong tradi-

tion of intense competition in the 83 years of the rivalry.
With the exception of the war years from 1943-45, the
two schools have faced each other every year since 1922.
The Utes dominated the series early on, avoiding
defeat until 1942, when the Cougars finally won, 12-7,
in Salt Lake City behind Coach Floyd Millet. The success
was short-lived, however, as it took BYU another 16
years to win another contest, 14-7, again in Salt Lake
City in 1958. In all, Utah won 34 of the first 40 con-
tests, four of which were ties.

In 1965, the momentum began to turn in BYU’s
favor. The Cougars took three in a row from the Utes
from 1965-67, and although Utah won the next four,

the competition had become much more interesting.

In 1972, the series took on an entirely different
feeling with the arrival of LaVell Edwards as BYU’s new
head coach. He took on a program that had gone 5-38-
4 (.149) against its fiercest rival and proceeded to post a
22-7 (.757) record against Utah in 29 years, including
wins in 15 of his first 16 meetings with the Utes. In the
Edwards era, the Cougars outscored the Utes an aver-
age of 34.9 to 20.9 per contest. Coach Edwards’ career
included winning streaks of nine (1979-87) and six
(1972-77) games against Utah.

The annual showdown generates considerable inter-

est throughout the state, making it a virtual certainty that
whatever stadium the teams play in will be sold out well
in advance. In all, seven of Utah’s top ten home crowds
before the expansion of Rice-Eccles Stadium were for
games against BYU.
  Meanwhile, 45 miles south in Provo, the grudge
match has accounted for five of the 20 largest crowds in
the history of LaVell Edwards Stadium.

One of the more memorable moments in the rivalry
was the 1989 contest in which the Cougars pasted the

Utes, 70-31, in Provo. It
was a game in which the
home team scored touch-
downs in each of its first
seven possessions and in
all eight with Ty Detmer
as quarterback. In a
move of mercy, Detmer
was replaced by backup
Sean Covey late in the
second quarter.

Then there were
the infamous back-to-
back, 34-31, losses at
the hands of the Utes in
1993 and 1994, giving
rebirth to a rivalry that
had become ridiculously
lopsided in the 80s and
early 90s.  In all, the Utes
won three in a row (1993-
95) under coach Ron
McBride.

The Cougars

responded to the new
aggression in 1996 with

BRaNDON DOMaN, 2000

In 2000 the Cougars led throughout the game only to fall behind 26-27 on a Matt
Nickel TD reception with 2 minutes to play. Behind the heart of Brandon Doman and
a little fate, BYU converted on a 4th down conversion with less than a minute to play.
Then Doman connected with senior Jonathan Pittman along the sideline before Luke
Staley took the ball inside Utah’s 10-yard line. From there Doman scored with 23 sec-
onds remaining on a quarterback sweep right to give BYU the miraculous come-from-
behind victory (photo above).

104

a dominating, 37-17, victory in Salt Lake City.  BYU
employed an extremely unconventional approach,
gaining 376 yards in 63 attempts on the ground and
attempting only 12 passes.

In 1998, a well-placed ESPN microphone picked up

an audible “clank” as the potential game-winning kick
bounced off the right goal post, allowing BYU to escape
with a, 26-24, victory — despite Ron McBride’s sideline
prayer, as captured on national television.

BYU let the Utes back into the game after having

seemingly put the game away in the fourth quarter,
allowing Utah to set up a 32-yard field goal to win the
game.  BYU fans gave credit to divine intervention as
the chip shot mysteriously hooked to the right at the last
possible moment.

In 2000 LaVell Edwards worked his magic for the
last time as Brandon Doman’s touchdown with 23 sec-
onds remaining finished an amazing last minute come-
back to send the legendary BYU coach out a winner.

Provo has been much more friendly to the Cougars

over the years, as BYU has posted a 13-16-1 record
overall at home but has only managed a 15-31-3 record
on the road against the Utes. Lately home field advan-
tage seems to have become less of a factor, as the Utes
won three straight at Edwards Stadium (1995, ‘97, ‘99)
and the Cougars won three in a row in Salt Lake City
(1996, 1998, 2000).

In 2001, BYU changed the trend in a miraculous,
come-from-behind victory in Provo. With less than four
minutes left in the game and down 11 points, BYU’s
hopes for a perfect season seemed dim. But Luke Staley
scored two touchdowns in just over two minutes to erase
the deficit and give the Cougars the 24-21 victory. The
game was far from over after Staley’s 30-yard touch-
down. Lance Rice led Utah on a final drive in the last
minute. Rice converted a key fourth-and-10 with a trick
play, advancing the ball to the BYU 30-yard line.  But on
the very next play, Jernaro Gilford stepped in front of a
Rice pass and intercepted the ball to seal the game

In 2002 Utah beat the Cougars for the first time
since 1994 in Salt Lake. The 13-6 Ute victory was not
without controversy. BYU quarterback Lance Pendleton
was stripped of the football on the goal line, but replays
later showed that his knee was down before the strip

In 2003 BYU held Utah to just three points on
a snowy day in Provo. Only problem was, BYU was
without offensive threat Reynaldo Brathwaite and its
top quarterbacks. BYU’s offense sputtered and the Utes
gave BYU its first shutout since 1975 (Ending an NCAA
record 361 consecutive games without a shutout).
Meanwhile the win also gave Utah its first outright
league championship since 1957. In 2005 BYU erased
a 21-point halftime deficit and forced the game into
overtime only to come up just short. John Beck’s fourth
down pass just missed the fingertips of Michael Reed as
BYU fell 41-34 in OT.

While BYU’s offense struggled in its first game under new offensive coordinator Robert Anae,
BYU’s defense played ACC foe Boston College tough. The defense, led by Dustin Gabriel
(above), kept the game close and held BC to just 10 points through three quarters.

BYU VS. BOSTON COLLEGE GAmE RECAP

Defense was the key for No. 22 Boston College as the Eagles handed BYU a 20-3 loss in

the 2005 season opener. The loss was the Cougars' first home, season-opening loss since

1997 when they fell 42-20 to Washington. John Beck completed 41-of-60 passes for 330

yards. The 41 completions were a career high for Beck and set LaVell Edwards Stadium and

Mountain West Conference records. Fahu Tahi led the way with 46 yards on eight receptions

and Curtis Brown was the leading rusher with eight carries for 37 yards. Boston College

struck first with 30 seconds remaining in the first quarter on a 10-play, 80-yard drive, end-

ing with a 14-yard touchdown pass over the middle from Quinton Porter to tight end Chris

Miller. The Eagles added three more points after Karim El Nokali blocked BYU's 47-yard

field goal attempt. Three plays later, the 35-yard drive ended with a 39-yard field goal by

Ryan Ohliger with three seconds remaining in the first half. Special teams came through for

the Cougars in the third quarter when Bryan Kehl blocked a punt, which set up a 35-yard

field goal by Jared McLaughlin with 10:16 remaining in the quarter. Boston College added

another touchdown when Porter and Miller connected in the fourth quarter. The Eagles put

the game away with a field goal by Ohliger with 4:25 remaining in the game.

(1-1)

Receiver Todd Watkins hauled in an acrobatic six-yard touchdown reception in the first half
against Eastern Illinois. Watkins finished the day with seven receptions for 93 yards in the
Cougars' 45-10 victory.

BYU VS. EASTERN ILLINOIS GAmE RECAP

BYU bounced back after a season-opening loss by posting a 45-10 victory over Eastern Illinois.
The Cougars sprinted to a 31-0 halftime lead before finishing off the Panthers and giving Bronco
Mendenhall his first victory as a head coach. The BYU rushing game was much improved from the
previous week, gaining 188 yards on 32 carries. Curtis Brown led the way with 18 carries for 112
yards and a touchdown. Fahu Tahi added 59 yards on nine carries and a touchdown. Todd Watkins
pulled in seven catches for 93 yards while John Beck finished the day completing 26-of-40 attempts
for 255 yards and three touchdowns. The 35-point win was the Cougars' most lopsided victory since
posting a 59-21 win over San Diego State on Oct. 27, 2001.

The Cougars wasted little time to show their resolve and determination, scoring on the first
drive of the game. Brown ran for a one-yard score, capping an eight-play, 67-yard drive. After the
Panthers fumbled a snap deep in their own territory, BYU scored another touchdown just two plays
later when John Beck picked up a one-yard touchdown to give the Cougars a 14-0. BYU added a
field in the first quarter following an interception by Dustin Gabriel. Senior receiver Todd Watkins got
into the act during the second quarter by scoring a pair of touchdowns, including a 31-yard reception
from Beck and an acrobatic 6-yard reception. Eastern Illinois took advantage of BYU miscues and
scored 10-unanswered before Tahi scored twice in the fourth quarter.

BYU VS. TCU GAmE RECAP

Despite a record setting performance, the Cougars lost to TCU, 51-50 in overtime and fell to

1-2 to start the season. John Beck threw for a MWC single-game record 517 yards and tied

an MWC record with five touchdown passes. Todd Watkins pulled in seven catches for 176

yards and tied a conference record with three touchdown receptions. Curtis Brown played

a solid game, compiling 172 total yards with nine catches for 92 yards and 23 rushes for

85 yards. It was not enough to hold off TCU as the Horned Frogs overcame the Cougars'

18-point lead and won in overtime. The comeback was fueled by backup quarterback Jeff

Ballard, who entered the game in the third quarter for an injured Tye Gunn. He finished

the game 8-of-12 for 150 yards and two touchdowns. The game lasted 4:42, the longest

game in BYU history. BYU got out to a quick start, putting up 21 points in the first quarter.

The Cougars scored on their first drive of the game on a 10-play 80-yard drive ending in a

seven-yard strike from Beck to Zac Collie, his first career touchdown. Beck and Collie con-

nected for the second time in the first quarter on a 37-yard touchdown pass, putting the

Cougars up 21-10. BYU led 41-29 in the fourth quarter before TCU scored 15 unanswered

points to take the lead. The Cougars then drove the ball 69 yards in eight plays and Jared

McLaughlin hit the game-tying, 39-yard field goal to send the game into overtime.

On the first play of overtime, Beck connected with Watkins for a 25-yard touchdown.

However, BYU fumbled the snap on the extra-point attempt and failed to convert. TCU then

put together a four-play, 25-yard drive that ended in a controversial touchdown call when

TCU's Rodgers appeared to fumble the ball on the goal line. After reviewing the play, the

officials stuck with the ruling on the field. TCU's Chris Manfredini put the extra point through
the uprights to give TCU the victory.

Senior running back Fahu Tahi led BYU in receptions with eight for 80 yards and rushing yards
with 33. Tahi also scored the Cougars lone touchdown in a game where BYU had difficulty
moving the football. San Diego State held BYU to just 305 total yards, including just 44 yards
rushing
BYU VS. SAN DIEGO STATE GAmE RECAP

San Diego State's Lynell Hamilton racked up 161 yards rushing with two touchdowns and the Aztecs
kept the Cougars offense in check and defeated BYU 31-10. The loss dropped the Cougars to 1-3
overall and 0-2 in the conference. A week after posting 50 points and 614 yards, the Cougars found
it difficult to move the ball against the Aztecs. SDSU held the Cougars to just 305 total yards on the
night, including only 44 yards rushing and 261 yards passing. Jonny Harline led the Cougars' with
123 yards on seven receptions, while Fahu Tahi had a team-high eight receptions for 80 yards. John
Beck was 25-of-49 for 261 yards, while Tahi also led the running game with 33 yards rushing and
one touchdown. After a scoreless first quarter, the Aztecs struck in the second quarter when Kevin
O'Connell scored on a six-yard run. O'Connell's touchdown run caped a 13-play drive that covered 80
yards. On the Aztecs' next drive, O'Connell found receiver Jeff Webb for a 50-yard completion, setting
up a 30-yard Garrett Palmer field goal that extended the lead to 10-0. With 4:51 left in the second
quarter, Bryan Kehl recovered an SDSU fumble at the Aztec's 40-yard line. Seven plays later, Jared
McLaughlin nailed a 33-yard field goal to get the Cougars on the board. San Diego State extended
its lead to 17-3 after Hamilton scored on a one-yard run right before the half. In the second half, the
Aztecs picked up where they left off, capitalizing on a Beck fumble and scoring seven plays later on
Brandon Bornes’ 4-yard touchdown run. Faced with a first-and-30 from the BYU 34-yard line, the
Cougars managed to get the offense rolling. Fahu Tahi scored on a one-yard run with 13:12 left in
the fourth quarter. San Diego State answered on the following drive with Hamilton scoring on a six-
yard run to give the Aztecs a commanding 31-10 lead.

The Cougars gave BYU head coach Bronco Mendenhall his first Division I-A victory, scoring

14 unanswered points in the fourth quarter to claim a 27-24 come-from-behind victory over

New Mexico. John Beck finished the night with a game-high 371 yards passing, completing

34-of-44 attempts with three touchdowns on the evening. Curtis Brown had a game-high

104 yards rushing while Jonny Harline had a team-high 10 receptions, equaling a season-

best 123 yards. Todd Watkins added six receptions for 68 yards, including a critical 29-yard

reception during the Cougars' game-winning drive. Brown totaled five catches for 35 yards.

All totaled 11 receivers were credited with at least one reception against the Lobos. The

defense, led by linebackers Cameron Jensen and Paul Walkenhorst held the Lobos scoreless

in the fourth quarter.

Down 24-19 with less than three minutes remaining in the game, Beck led the

Cougars on a five-play, 80-yard drive that ended with Matt Allen catching the 23-yard,

game-winning touchdown with just 1:40 remaining in the game. Curtis Brown converted

on the two-point try to extend the lead to three at 27-24. A tough defensive stand forced

the Lobos to turn the ball over on downs with 45 seconds remaining, sealing the Cougars'

first Mountain West victory of the season. With just under 13:00 minutes left in the game,

BYU got a much-needed break when Markell Staffieri recovered a McKamey fumble on the

Cougars' own seven-yard line. The Cougars marched 93 yards in 13 plays, scoring on a

nine-yard run by Brown to pull within five points with 7:15 remaining in the game. Early in

The Cougars relied on the rushing game in their 24-14 win over Colorado State at Edwards
Stadium. Led by Curtis Brown (above) and Fahu Tahi, BYU amassed 274 yards rushing on 52
attempts. Brown accumulated 147, Tahi had 86 and John Beck added 37.

BYU VS. COLORADO STATE GAmE RECAP

The one-two punch of running backs Curtis Brown and Fahu Tahi was too much for Colorado State
as the Cougars rolled past the Rams 24-14 on Homecoming night and handied Colorado State its
first conference loss of the season. BYU racked up a 274 yards rushing, the most since BYU ran for
410 yards against Colorado State in 2001. Brown led the way with 147 yards and two touchdowns
on 31 carries. Tahi gained 86 yards on 14 carries. The defense came up big for the Cougars, holding
the Rams to only 30 rushing yards. The Cougar defense also had a season-high four sacks in the
game with Justin Luettgerodt leading the way with two and a half.

The Cougars struck first with 12:27 left in the second quarter on a four-yard touchdown run

by Curtis Brown ending a 10-play 65-yard drive. BYU put seven more points on the scoreboard with
1:32 left in the second quarter when Daniel Coats made a spectacular catch in the back of the end
zone on a two yard pass from John Beck, the only pass attempt in the quarter. The Cougars added
three more on a 28-yard field goal by Jared McLaughlin with 6:40 remaining in the third quarter to
go up 17-0. Colorado State scored consecutive touchdowns to cut the lead to three at 17-14. The
Cougars answered with a 14-play, 76-yard drive that ended on a two-yard touchdown run by Brown
with 2:07 left in the game, putting the game out of reach for the Rams.

Despite keeping the score close for much of three quarters, the Cougars were unable to

contain the Notre Dame air attack and fell 49-23 to the Irish. Irish quarterback Brady

Quinn threw for a Notre Dame record six touchdowns, including a school record four TD's

to receiver Maurice Stovall. John Beck completed 26-of-45 attempts for 317 yards and two

touchdowns, marking his eighth-career 300-yard game. Senior running back Fahu Tahi led

the Cougars with 63 yards on the ground, and added 73 yards receiving. The BYU defense

held the Irish to just 44 yards rushing, limiting running back Darius Walker to just 17 yards

on the night. Safety Corby Hodgkiss was credited with the Cougars' only sack of the game,

stopping Quinn for a four-yard loss. The defense had four tackles for a combined loss of 20

yards. Jared McLaughlin connected on a 44-yard field goal on BYU’s opening drive to give

the Cougars a 3-0 lead. The Irish took the lead on the ensuing series on a 10-yard pass

from Quinn under pressure to Stovall. The Cougars responded to go up 10-7 when Beck

hooked up with Harline for a 12-yard touchdown to complete a 51-yard drive. Notre Dame

regained the lead for good at 14-10 on Quinn's 14-yard pass to Samardzija. The Irish extend-

ed their lead to 28-10 in the second quarter before the Cougars made a run and cut the lead

to 28-23. Beck scored on a 10-yard run to trim the lead to 28-17. After a Notre Dame punt,

BYU cut the lead to 28-23 after Beck connected with Watkins in the endzone on a fourth-

down attempt from the six-yard line. The Irish then responded with three touchdowns.

For the second consecutive year, quarterback John Beck had an impressive passing per-
formance against the Falcons. Beck completed 31-of-43 attempts for 383 yards and three
touchdowns. This was Beck’s fourth 300-yard passing game on the season.

BYU VS. AIR FORCE GAmE RECAP

scoring sUmmAry

The Cougar offense racked up 300 yards rushing and 383 yards passing for a season-high 683
yards en route to a 62-41 shootout win over Air Force. The game marked the first time since 1989
against Utah the Cougars had over 300 yards rushing and passing in the same game. The 683
yards marked the most yards the Cougars have racked up in a game since the 2001 season. In addi-
tion, the team picked up 39 first downs, equaling a team record. Curtis Brown ran for a career-high
219 yards and scored a personal-best four rushing touchdowns while John Beck completed 31-of43
pass attempts for 383 yards, three touchdowns and one interception. Michael Reed caught five pass-
es for 103 yards and his first-career touchdown. Air Force’s backup quarterback Adam Fitch entered
the game for an injured Shawn Carney and threw for 265 yards and five touchdowns.

The Cougars took a 14-0 lead in the first quarter on a 14-yard touchdown reception by Reed

and a 12-yard run by Nathan Meikle, which came immediately following a fumble recovery by Justin
Luettgerodt. The Falcons scored in the second quarter before BYU added two more touchdowns, the
first a six-yard run by Brown and the second a 14-yard pass from Beck to Fahu Tahi, to make the
score 28-7. In the third quarter, Jared McLaughlin notched two field goals and Daniel Coats had a
15-yard touchdown reception, making the score 41-7. Air Force scored their second touchdown of
the game on the last play of the third quarter with Fitch and Jason Brown hooking up for the second
time on a 57-yard pass. The Falcons made it a game in the fourth, cutting the gap to two touch-
downs at 48-34 and scoring a total of 27 points. The Cougars added 21 points of their own during
the final frame as Curtis Brown scored three touchdowns in the quarter.

BYU

UNLV

BYU eclipsed the 50-point plateau for the second-consecutive game in defeating UNLV 55-

14 to improve to 5-4 overall and 4-2 in Mountain West Conference action. BYU held the

Rebels to a season-low two yards rushing, the fourth time the Cougars held their opponents

under 100 yards rushing, and picked off three Rebel passes which led to 21 points. BYU

also registered five sacks, led by Justin Maddux who had 3.0 sacks for a combined loss

of 22 yards. Offensively, the Cougars rolled up 224 yards rushing and added 267 yards

passing. John Beck passed for 267 yards and three touchdowns while completing 23-of-34

attempts. BYU’s leading receiver was Jonny Harline, who had 80 yards and one touchdown

on six receptions. Freshman Wayne Latu had 93 yards and one touchdown on 15 carries

while Curtis Brown scored a rushing and a receiving touchdown.

The Cougars got on the board first for the fifth straight game, scoring on a one-yard

run by Fahu Tahi. UNLV tied the score in the second quarter when Greg Estandia caught

a five-yard touchdown pass. BYU went back ahead on the following drive on a 45-yard

field goal by Jared McLaughlin. From there the Cougars broke the game open as Justin

Luettgerodt picked off a pass on the Rebel’s next possession and returned it to the UNLV

24-yard line. Several plays later Beck hit Todd Watkins for an 11-yard score. Another BYU

interception, by Kayle Buchanan, got the ball back for the Cougars who scored six plays

later on a one-yard run by Curtis Brown. BYU added another field goal before the half to

take a 27-7 lead into the locker room. The Cougars continued to dominate in the second

half, outscoring the Rebels 28-7 as Brown, Tahi, Harline and Latu all scored.

C. Brown 2-24; Reed 2-12; Meikle 1-19; Allen 1-11; Collie 1-9; Tahi 1-8;

Senior linebacker Justin Leuttgerodt emerged as the defensive hero as he recovered with three
fumble and had a fourth-quarter interception that sealed the Cougars' victory. Luettgerodt
accounted for four of BYU’s five takeaways as it became bowl eligible with it sixth win.

BYU VS. WYOmING GAmE RECAP

BYU scored 21 points off five Wyoming turnovers, defeating the Cowboys 35-21 in Laramie. The
win put the Cougars at 6-4 overall and 5-2 in the Mountain West, ensuring the Cougars their first
winning regular season since 2001 and giving the team the necessary number of wins to become
bowl eligible for the first time over that three-year span. The Cougars also clinched at least a share
of second place in the league standings. Justin Leuttgerodt emerged as the defensive hero as he
recovered with three fumble and had a fourth-quarter interception that sealed the Cougars' victory.
Offensively, John Beck completed 20-of-29 attempts to 11 different receivers for 179 yards and
two touchdowns. Jonny Harline had a team-high four receptions for 28 yards and two touchdown
receptions. Curtis Brown tied the BYU record for career 100-yard games, picking up the 10th of his
career. Brown had a game-high 153 yards on 25 carries and two touchdowns.

Wyoming took the opening drive 80 yards in just five plays to take an early 7-0 lead as Corey

Bramlet connected with Jovon Bouknight for a 21-yard touchdown. On the following drive, the
Cougars used a balanced attack to even the score, driving 84 yards on five rushing plays and five
passing plays. Brown capped the drive with a six-yard touchdown run. Luettgerodt got the ball back
for BYU as he recovered a fumble and Beck found Harline for a 14-yard touchdown pass to give the
Cougars a 14-7 advantage. Wynel Seldon answered with a 69-yard touchdown run to tie the score
at 14. BYU responded with a methodical 17-play scoring drive that covered 89 yards and took 8:21
off the clock and ended with Brown scoring on a six-yard run to give BYU the 21-14 lead. Lettgerodt
was credited with his second fumble recovery on Wyoming’s next drive and seven plays later, Fahu
Tahi strolled into the endzone to give BYU a 28-14 lead. The Cowboys scored first in the second half
and threatened to tie the score at 28 but Luettgerodt came up with another fumble recovery that led
to another scoring drive as Harline make a spectacular one-handed grab in the back of the endzone
to make the score 35-21.
